NAVAJO N  
NAVAJO COUNTY PUBLIC WORKS DEPARTMENT 
PLANNING & ZONING 
 
 
STAFF REPORT  
BOARD OF SUPERVISORS 
 
Hearing Date: January 28, 2020 
Case No:  19-78 
Action:   Final Plat Extension 
Applicant/Owner: Pacific Holt Corporation 
Project Name: Mesa Valley Plat Extension 
PARCEL INFORMATION  
 Address: 4150 and 4108 Boyse Road  
 
 APN:  109-33-040E & F, 109-01-005C 
 
 Legal Desc: Township 18 North, Range 20E, Section 33 and Township 17 North, Range 20
   East, Section 4 of the Gila and Salt River Meridian in the Holbrook area.  
 
 District: III 
 
 Area:  Holbrook, AZ 
 
 Parcel Size: Approximately 37.46, 59.55, & 9.57 Acres 
 
STATED REASON FOR REQUEST:  
 
The applicant has requested a time extension on their previously granted Final Plat approval for Mesa 
Hills Estates, Phase 1. 
 
GENERAL CHARACTER OF NEIGHBORHOOD:  
 
The subject property is surrounded by both vacant and single-family residential properties within the 
A-General and Special Development Zoning Districts.  The subject property is located just off of I-40 
on Boyse Road. 
 
BACKGROUND:

<!-- page 131 -->
The original Final Plat for Mesa Hills was approved by the Navajo County Board of Supervisors, 
through Resolution No. 09-13.  The initial approval of the Final Plat was subject to the following 
conditions: 
 
1. Approval is conditioned upon all subdivision improvements being substantially completed 
within 24 months after the date of the Board of Supervisors approval (or any extension thereof 
which may be granted by the Board of Supervisors, as evidenced by the recordation of a 
resolution of extension in the Office of the Navajo County Recorder). 
 
2. Upon approval of the Final Plat the subdivision roads will not be accepted into the Navajo 
County road maintenance system until they are brought up to meet the Navajo County road 
maintenance standard by approval of the Superintendent of Streets, at which time the roads 
will automatically be accepted for maintenance by Navajo County with no further action 
required. 
 
3. The Final Plat document and CC&R’s will not be recorded until all subdivision improvements 
have been completed to the satisfaction of the Navajo County Engineer and receipt of a 
financial assurances agreement for work within the Navajo County Right-of-Way is approved 
by the Navajo County Attorney’s office and signed by the Chairman of the Board of 
Supervisors. 
 
On September 12th, 2017, a Final Plat Extension was granted for Mesa Hills Estates by the Navajo 
County Board of Supervisors, subject to the following conditions. 
 
1. Approval is conditioned upon all subdivision improvements being substantially completed 
within 24 months after the date of the P&Z Commission time extension approval.  As this is 
the third (3rd) extension, if ground is not broken, grading of site and construction of 
improvements begun, it is recommended that the Final Plat expire.  If the applicant desires to 
appeal the expiration, they can petition to go before the Board of Supervisors, by submitting 
request to the Clerk of the Board. 
 
2. Upon approval of the Final Plat, the subdivision roads will not be accepted into the Navajo 
County road maintenance system until they are brought up to meet the Navajo County road 
maintenance standard by approval of the Superintendent of Streets, at which time the roads 
will automatically be accepted for maintenance by Navajo County with no further action 
required.   
 
3. The Final Plat document and CC&R’s will not be recorded until all subdivision improvements 
have been completed to the satisfaction of the Navajo County Engineer and receipt of a 
financial assurances agreement for work within the Navajo County Right-of-Way is approved 
by the Navajo County Attorney’s office and signed by the Chairman of the Board of 
Supervisors. 
 
At the Planning and Zoning Commission Hearing held on October 17th, 2019, the Navajo County 
Planning and Zoning Commission voted unanimously to recommend denial of this Final Plat 
Extension to the Navajo County Board of Supervisors. 
 
PROJECT DESCRIPTION:  
 
The relevant Final Plat has already been approved by the Navajo County Board of Supervisors in

<!-- page 132 -->
2013.  With this approval, there was a condition stating that all subdivision improvements be 
substantially complete within 24 months after the date of the Board of Supervisors approval.  This 
deadline has already been approved for 3, separate 2-year periods.  The applicant for this project is 
requesting a final extension of their Final Plat approved in 2013 that would allow for one further 2-
year extension on their time to complete subdivision improvements, causing the Plat to expire if this 
condition is not met.   
 
ZONING DISTRICTS: 
ARTICLE 9 – (R1-43) SINGLE-FAMILY RESIDENTIAL ZONING DISTRICT 
    43,000 SQUARE FEET PER DWELLING UNIT 
 
Section 901 – Purpose.  
 
The principal purpose of this zoning district is to conserve and protect single-family residential 
development where minimum lots of not less than forty-three thousand (43,000) square feet in 
area are suitable and appropriate taking into consideration existing conditions, including 
present use of land, present lot sizes, future land use needs, and the availability of public 
utilities.  Principal uses permitted in this zoning district include single-family dwellings, 
churches, schools, parks, playgrounds, and other community facilities. 
 
COMPREHENSIVE PLAN/AREA PLAN:  
Comprehensive Plan 
 
There are no goals, policies, or actions in Navajo County’s Comprehensive Plan that relate to this 
request. 
 
FINDINGS OF FACT: 
 
1. The item has been properly noticed and posted in compliance with Arizona Revised Statutes 
and Article 29.  
 
This item was posted in the October 23rd, 2019 edition of the Holbrook Tribune.  Additionally, the 
property was posted, and postcards were sent to neighbors within 300 feet on October 28th, 2019 
to meet all standards laid out by Arizona Revised Statutes and Article 29 of the Navajo County 
Zoning Ordinance. 
 
2. The proposal conforms with the Navajo County Zoning Ordinance, other ordinances and 
regulations of the County, and the Arizona Revised Statutes. 
 
In the Past, it was determined that the subdivision that had its Final Plat approved conformed with 
all ordinances and standards, so a Final Plat extension should do nothing to change this 
conformance. 
 
3. The proposal implements the goals, objectives, and policies of the Navajo County 
Comprehensive Plan. 
 
This specific request has no goals, policies, or objectives that are referred to in the Navajo County 
Comprehensive Plan.

<!-- page 133 -->
4. The proposal is compatible with the permissible uses in the zoning district in which the 
property is located. 
 
The proposal that was previously approved is looking to establish a single-family residential 
subdivision, which is acceptable in the R1-43 Zoning District. 
 
5. The proposal is compatible with the current and likely future uses of properties in the 
vicinity of the proposed use.  
 
Properties in the vicinity of the proposed subdivision being extended are either currently vacant or 
single-family residential, which is in line with the proposal. 
 
6. The proposal will have minimal adverse effects on the public health, safety, and general 
welfare of properties in the vicinity of the proposal. 
 
In the past, it was determined that there would be minimal adverse effects to public health, safety, 
and general welfare of properties in the vicinity of the proposal, so an extension on the Final Plat 
should do nothing to change this.

WHEREAS, an application for a Final Plat Extension was duly filed on August 15,

zoned R1-43; and  
 
WHEREAS, the application concerns the following real property in Navajo County: 
APN: 109-33-040E, 109-33-040F, and 109-01-005C, which are portions of Township 18 North, 
Range 20 East, Section 33 and Township 17 North, Range 20 East, Section 4 of the Gila and Salt 
River Meridian in the Holbrook area; and

Meeting held at the Navajo County Board of Supervisors Chambers, Holbrook, Arizona – 
Time:  6:12 to 9:37. 
 
Chairman Teetsel called the meeting of the Navajo County Planning & Zoning 
Commission to order and explained the meeting procedures to the public.  Mr. Teetsel 
then led the pledge of Allegiance. 
 
ITEM #1 – PLEDGE OF ALLEGIANCE. 
 
ITEM #2 – REVIEW OF AGENDA BY COMMISSIONERS. 
 
ITEM #3 – CALL TO PUBLIC FOR ITEMS NOT ON THE AGENDA. 
 
ITEM #3– CASE # 19-81 SPECIAL USE PERMIT, DISTRICT III: Discussion and possible 
action on a request by Deryl Cooley for a Special Use Permit to allow for a private, family 
cemetery on a portion of APN 403-81-002A, a portion of Township 14 North, Range 23 
East, Section 31 of the Gila and Salt River Meridian in the Snowflake area.  Withdrawn at 
applicant’s request 
 
It was at this time that Commissioner Randy Murph recused himself from the following two 
items as they were a conflict of interest to him.

<!-- page 138 -->
ITEM #4 – CASE #19-78 FINAL PLAT EXTENSION, DISTRICT III:  Discussion and 
possible action on a request by Pacific Holt Corporation for a Final Plat Extension for Mesa 
Hills Estates on APN 109-33-040E, 109-33-040F, & 109-01-005C, which are portions of 
Township 18 North, Range 20E, Section 33 and Township 17 North, Range 20 East, 
Section 4 of the Gila and Salt River Meridian in the Holbrook area. 
 
Mr. Cooper introduced the item, directing the Commission to the map for the area of the 
final plat, showing the letter the applicant submitted requesting the extension and said that 
the plat was originally approved back in 2013, with a zone change approved in 2008 to 
allow the final plat process. The extension is required because infrastructure has not been 
started or completed in a timely manner and they still wish to develop the land at some 
point. The final completion of the subdivision’s standards were presented, and the 
extension would keep the recommendations and stipulations that were approved in 2013 
in place. Mr. Cooper noted that the applicant was not present. 
 
Chairman Teetsel  asked the Commission if they had questions for staff, and 
Commissioner Rhoades asked if it was the 4
th extension. Mr. Cooper said that it was the 
4th extension period, with previous extensions being approved. 
 
Commissioner Rhoades  asked why it was taking so long for the subdivision to be 
developed. Ms. Phillips replied that the applicant has been hoping there would be some 
growth in the area before they put any more work into the project, as the engineering plans 
they first submitted were substantial. When staff questioned this-the applicant requested 
one more extension.
  
 
Chairman Teetsel asked what the applicant was expecting to change in 24 months that 
would warrant approval for this extension. Sandra said the Applicant was watching growth, 
hoping things would turn around in the market. 
 
Commissioner Rhoades asked about the two small parcels that were not a part of the 
plat owned by a different property owner, and how that affected the project. Staff 
responded that the extension was only going to be for phase one of the project, which 
affected sixteen lots and did not reach the area of the two separate parcels. 
 
Chairman Teetsel asked what the downside to this was if it was denied, if they had to 
start all over and get new engineering studies to meet new standards. Ms. Phillips said 
that they would have to get new engineering studies and work to meet updated standards. 
 
Commissioner Howe asked staff if the applicant indicated that there would be a hardship 
if they did not receive the extension.  Ms. Phillips replied that staff had not asked such of 
them, and it was not indicated that there would be a hardship from the applicant. 
 
The Chairman addressed the audience, asking if any wished to speak in favor, and finding 
none, asked if the audience wanted to address the board in opposition. 
  
Vera Wiggen approached to speak and stated that this subdivision has been talked about 
for 12 years, and with two major businesses leaving the area very soon, growth is unlikely, 
and both Joseph City and Holbrook had plenty of room for new inhabitants.

<!-- page 139 -->
Commissioner Howe  asked if the taxes would change with the development of the 
subdivision. Ms. Phillips said they’d likely increase; Commissioner Howe further asked if 
the final plat and roads have been approved, Sandr a replied that they had, but that no 
ground had been broken.  Commissioner Howe said that this might be an issue, for them 
to sit on land where they’d disturbed the ground and made it unstable from a half-hearted 
attempt to develop the area, leading to potential flooding issues and so forth. 
 
Commissioner Rhoades asked if this was a “final” final plat extension, asked if they didn’t 
do anything in 24 months, where would it go from there? Ms. Phillips said they’d either 
abandon the project or apply for another extension. 
 
Chuck asked how staff felt about all this, Staff responded that they were neutral. 
 
Chairman Teetsel called for a motion.  Commissioner DeCross made a motion to 
recommend Denial to the Board.  Commissioner Rhoades seconded the motion.  
Motion carried, 4-0. 
 
 
ITEM #5 – CASE #19-79 FINAL PLAT EXTENSION, DISTRICT III:  Discussion and 
possible action on a request by Pacific Holt Corporation for a Final Plat Extension for 
Perkins Valley Estates on APN 109-33-039, a portion of Township 18 North, Range 20 
East, Section 33 of the Gila and Salt River Meridian in the Holbrook area. 
 
Chairman Teetsel  and Commissioner Rhoades  asked if the arguments from the 
previous item were the same or similar for this project, as it was the same applicant. Mr. 
Cooper replied that it was indeed the same argument, including the reasons behind not 
developing the project in the past twelve years. Staff did show the commission the area 
and extent of the project as well as where they had posted.  
 
The Chairman called to the public for comment for or against. 
 
Ms. Vera stood back up to speak to the commission to say that the water table and flooding 
was an issue with this project. Chairman Teetsel said that was something they would 
have to work out with the building department. 
 
Chairman Teetsel called for a motion.  Commissioner Howe made a motion to 
recommend Denial to the Board.  Commissioner DeCross seconded the motion.  
Motion carried 4 -0.  
 
ITEM #6 – CASE #19-88 SPECIAL USE PERMIT, DISTRICT IV: Discussion and possible 
action on a request by Samara LLC for a Special Use Permit to allow for the construction 
and operation of a landscape supply store on APN: 208-14-002U, a portion of Township 
11 North, Range 19 East, Section 15 of the Gila and Salt River Meridian in the Clay

ITEM #7 – CASE #19-82 SPECIAL USE PERMIT, DISTRICT III: Discussion and possible 
action on a request by Invenergy for a Special Use Permit to allow for the construction 
and operation of a 400 MW solar energy generation and storage facility on approximately 
3,000 acres on a portion of APN 107-01-023, 107-05-031C, 107-05-031D, & 110-15-001D, 
which are portions of Township 18 North, Range 18 East, Section 36, Township 18 North,

<!-- page 140 -->
Range 19 East, Sections 30, 31, & 32, & Township 17 North, Range 19 East, Section 5 of 
the Gila and Salt River Meridian in the Joseph City area. 
 
Commissioner McVicker recused himself from this item as he felt this was a conflict of 
interest. 
 
Mr. Cooper introduced the project to the commission and clarified that the project would 
have 300-megawatt nameplate capacity, plus a 100-megawatt battery storage facility. The 
panels would be about ten feet tall, they’d have collectors, substations and storage 
facilities on the site.  He also noted that this was in the Aztec Area Plan and met the 
standards the Board of Supervisors laid out for this area.  
 
Ms. Phillips added that they were going to add in corridors for where the new lines would 
connect to the grid, and Mr. Cooper said that they had an example for the type of facilities 
that they had in mind for Navajo County and showed the Commission what had been 
provided. 
 
Staff had also included pictures to show the Commission the terrain; because of droughts 
there were some concerns about dust as there was no vegetation to hold it down, and the 
ground beneath said dust was incredibly hard. How the dust would be addressed as well 
as road access would be managed would be discussed further by the applicant. 
 
The applicant held a public meeting in Joseph City on May 15
th.  They are working with 
staff and the fire department on a fire plan.  Mr. Cooper then outlined the notification 
boundaries and said that the applicant had some concerns with the requirements Staff 
had drafted; the applicant was available to answer questions. 
 
The Chairman then asked the Commission if they had questions for Staff, 
 
Commissioner Rhoades commented on the effects toward wildlife, and asked about the 
five-year construction period, if that was more for initial construction or if there would be 
an expansion of the project. Staff confirmed that it was a possibility and they were keeping 
options open, and that the applicant was planning for a wildlife corridor.  
 
The Chairman asked the applicant to come forward to speak. 
 
Quentin Stuart spoke to the Commission, thanking staff for working closely with Invenergy. 
He introduced his company, Invenergy, based in Chicago and Denver. 
 
The overview of the project indicated that they were hoping to have a generating capacity 
of up to 400-megawatts and that the applicant was working with the State and Aztec Land 
Company for lease of the land.  He gave a very  brief overview of how their facility would 
work, and how the battery stations would help create a median for day and night use.  
 
They anticipated to generate around 200 construction jobs, and 3-4 permanent jobs to 
monitor the site. They were anticipating a revenue boost in taxes for the area and said 
that they would have a very minimal impact on the area, they would hardly need to clean 
the panels, and that no water would be needed on site.  In terms of fire safety, they’ve 
been working with the fire chief Rusty DeSpain to create plans, as well as working with 
the landowner to ensure that if they needed to decommission the project that they would

<!-- page 141 -->
be able to remove the facility in a timely manner.  They have completed the studies for 
environmental needs and are motivated to be finished by 2021. 
 
The commission discussed with staff and the applicant about the dust control and road 
maintenance on McLaws. Mr. Bess noted that they would be getting pictures via drone to 
compare before and after for records and maintenance purposes. 
 
The Applicants also noted that: 
 They had no plans to cover the ground, the shade from the panels typically kept 
vegetation from growing too large- how ever they would assess hiring local 
companies for groundskeeping. 
 The posts for the panels would be driven and would not require concrete. 
 The lifespan of the project is about 20-25 years. 
 They could do a glare analysis for the nearby airport. 
 There were emergency plans in place if tariffs made materials difficult to come by. 
 The housing for the batteries would be a warehouse on concrete slab with air 
conditioning, and removal of the battery banks would be done at the end of the life 
of the plant. 
 
Chairman Teetsel called for those who wished to speak in favor to the project, 
 
Mr. Steve Brophy came up to speak to the commission in favor of the project, noting that 
as the land owner he had interests in keeping the applicant straight when it came to 
maintenance of the land, and highly approved of the low impact to the environment. 
 
Mr. J.R. DeSpain to speak against the project, his company holds the current grazing 
lease from Aztec Land and Cattle Co.  His first complaint was that size was an issue as 
his cattle company was losing grazing land for this project. His second issue was with the 
dust this project would generate, as well as the glare. He also noted that he was concerned 
about the use of pesticides to keep the weeds down, as this might affect the grazing land.  
 
Cindy DeSpain also came up to speak that they had concerns as there is a lot of Aztec 
land that they hold grazing leases on, and they had asked for certain areas to remain 
untouched. Otherwise her concerns were the same as her brother’s. 
 
The Commission, staff and applicant then discussed various changes to the 
recommendations that staff had been unable to agree upon with the applicant beforehand 
and came to the conclusion after discussing it with counsel that they could recommend an 
approval to the Board of Supervisors with the condition that the finer details be worked out 
with staff and counsel prior to going before the Board. 
 
Chairman called for a motion.  Commissioner Howe made a motion to recommend 
Conditional Approval to the Board, with the exception that the items 3.d, 3.i, and 10 be 
finalized with Staff, Counsel, and the Applicant prior to going before the Board of 
Supervisors.  Commissioner DeCross seconded the motion.  Motion carried, 3-1. (Murph) 
 
ITEM #8 – CASE #19-83 SPECIAL USE PERMIT, DISTRICT II: Discussion and possible 
action on a request by Chevelon Butte RE LLC for a Special Use Permit to allow for the 
construction and operation of a portion of a 477 MW maximum capacity wind generation 
facility on approximately 3,650 acres on a portion of APN: 110-01-004 and 110-01-005, a

<!-- page 142 -->
portion of Township 15 North, Range 15 East, Sections 22, 26, 27, 34, 35, & 36 of the Gila 
and Salt River Meridian in the Chevelon Canyon area. 
 
Mr. Cooper introduced the project to the commission and stated that this is a 477-
megawatt nameplate capacity, joint jurisdiction project between Navajo and Coconino 
County, and then displayed the area of the project, most of which is in Coconino County.  
There are 164 wind turbines being proposed, however only 8 will be in Navajo County 
territory, he also pointed out the basic statistics for the turbines, such as the distance, 
access through the forest service 504 and Hutch roads.  Main construction access would 
be off route 99.  Mr. Cooper presented a letter from the landowner that was providing the 
lease, and the current holder of the grazing lease was O’Haco Land and Cattle Co., they 
were working with the State for easement access. Staff noted that the applicant had 
provided above and beyond State standards for their environmental studies and that they 
would all be in line with the requirements for such. Ms. Phillips pointed out the highlights 
of a sound study submitted by the applicant that indicated the area of effect, stating that 
the Wind Ordinance currently allows a decibel level of 45. Staff then provided the 
Commission with visualization simulations from the applicant to show the Commission the 
visual impact the turbines would have. The project is close to State Route 99, but not as 
close to existing residences.  The views will be impacted and that’s the only 
Comprehensive Plan policy this project would go against.  The applicant had public 
meetings within both Coconino and Navajo County.  One such meeting was held in 
Flagstaff on July 15
th with the Hopi Tribe. Mr. Cooper stated the area of notification was 
greater than those required by State Statutes, with all neighbors within one mile notified, 
and that the meetings were put into the public notice in the paper. They also were working 
with the fire department with similar concerns as the previous project. Mr. Cooper noted 
that staff recommends approval of the project, subject to conditions. 
 
Commissioner DeCross asked if Coconino County has heard this item yet.  Mr. Cooper 
said no and that they hear it in December. 
 
Commissioner Howe asked where the nearest home was, Ms. Phillips said it was 2.5 
miles away from the nearest turbine. 
   
Bill Bess, the County Engineer, addressed the Commission briefly and said that the 504-
forest road has a part that is county-maintained, however, some of it was forest service 
only, and that some areas were two-track dirt roads, and that other roads were private. 
Mr. Bess also mentioned that the County maintains a portion of State Route 99 after ten 
miles outside of Winslow and will add that into our monitoring program as with the previous 
item. 
 
Staff noted the applicant was available for questions, and Chairman Teetsel asked the 
applicant to speak. 
 
Mr. Terrance Unrein, the applicant, introduced his company and showed the Commission 
some of their projects throughout the state. He described the distance of the project from 
Flagstaff and showed the Commission the impact of the complete project, he wanted to 
touch on the public outreach program, and said that they have put up a website and sent 
letters to 200 property owners in both Coconino and Navajo County and held public 
outreach meetings in Winslow and Flagstaff. They have done what they feel is an 
adequate job reaching out to the public and other entities.  He said that they had 
Commission meetings with Coconino near the end of the month for a planning session,

<!-- page 143 -->
but that the December date for the meeting was to accommodate for the Thanksgiving 
holiday. They have done deep research and studies in environmental impact.  He has 
completed class three pedestrian cultural research studies on the State land and said that 
they have done so voluntarily to ensure protection for the archeological sites that they’ve 
identified. They also presented a map showing that the area they have chosen is the best 
possible wind corridor.  He also wished to point out that this project was intended to keep 
the land as cattle grazing land.  Mr. Unrein pointed out that these things are huge, standing 
at 754’, They will be seen for miles, but they chose a highly rural area with the closest 
property owner in Coconino being eight miles away.  They are trying to be open with this 
project and are excited to be in Navajo County, as they are interested in owning the project 
rather than building it and selling it off. 
 
Mr. Unrein said he was ready with a team to address any questions or concerns, and the 
Commission discussed with them aspects of the project that would be worked out in the 
permitting process, as well as went over the few issues they had with Staff’s 
Requirements, coming to the same conclusion to work out the details as with agenda item 
number 7 with staff and counsel. 
 
The Chairman called for a motion.   
 
Commissioner Howe made a motion to recommend Approval to the Board with the 
condition that items 5.i and 13 are to be revised as needed by staff, the applicant and 
counsel.  Commissioner Rhoades seconded the motion.  Motion carried, 3-2. 
(Commissioners DeCross and Murph.)

Title: Wendover Pines - District IV - Request Approval of Master Site Plan
Submitted For: John Osgood, Public Works Director 
Submitted By: Sandra Phillips, Assistant County Engineer/Flood Control Manager
Department: Public Works Division: Planning & Zoning
Presented By: Sandra Phillips & Cody Cooper
Motion before the Board:
PUBLIC HEARING: Consideration and adoption of Resolution Number 07-2020 
approving/denying request for approval of Master Site Plan for Wendover Pines, for up to 450
residential units, a commercial-retail space, and open space on APN 207-04-006A and
207-04-007B located west of Heber in Township 12 North, Range 16 East, Section 15.
Background:
The proposed project area is just under 309 acres, with a total of up to 450 residential units.  The
project is directly surrounded by U.S. Forest Service land and is across State Route 260 from High
Country Pines, a Special Development zoned project.  There is a mix of lot sizes proposed along
with approximately 14 acre park/amenity and 5.7 acres commercial with 1.9 acres multi-family. 
The lot sizes will vary from 7,000 sq.ft to almost 2 acres.  See attached Site Plan.  The applicant
plans to install a water distribution system and wastewater collection system to serve this
community.  As stated in the narrative, native vegetation and landscape are great assets to the
project and the goal is to preserve and incorporate as many as possible into the development plan
as appropriate and practical.  This proposed development is in conformance with the requirements
of both the Navajo County Comprehensive Plan and Heber/Overgaard Area Plan.
The applicant held a well attended public meeting at the Heber/Overgaard Fire Station on
December 11, 2019 at 6pm.  It was standing room only with lots of residents being turned away. 
County Staff attended the meeting.  The primary concerns that were voiced were further
investigated and presented to the P&Z Commission.  See attached draft of the meeting minutes for
further information.  The Commission recommended approval of this Master Site Plan to the
Board with a unanimous 6-0 vote.
Recommendation: 
Staff recommends approval of this Master Site Plan of Wendover Pines.

Wendover Pines  Special Development Zone Change Narrative 
Page 1 
 
 
1.0 INTRODUCTION AND PURPOSE OF REQUEST 
On Behalf of Hunn Development and the owner, RVi Planning + Landscape Architecture is requesting 
a zone change for approximately 295-acres of property in unincorporated Navajo County located on 
State Route 260 and Forest Road 504 in the Heber/Overgaard area of unincorporated Navajo County 
from A-General to Special Development (SD) zoning.  The request is to allow for the development of 
Wendover Pines, a master planned community that will include up to 450 residential units of varying 
product types, lot sizes and densities, commercial, and na tural and improved open space. The plan 
will also define custom development standards in accordance with the Special Development Zone 
provisions to allow for a creative, attractive and functional master planned community that responds 
to the natural character of the property. 
The proposed master planned community will create a nature themed neighborhood that focuses on 
the inherent natural beauty of the land, including t he existing forest, natural wash corridors and 
rolling topography.  The existing vegetation and terrain will be preserved to the highest degree 
possible with roads and home sites designed and orientated to incorporate clusters of Ponderosa 
stands.  Community trails will follow the rolling terrain and will embrace the natural vegetation of the 
region.  Residents and visitors to the community will feel as though they are within an old homestead 
within a forest. 
 
2.0 EXISTING CONDITIONS ANALYSIS 
2.1 PROPERTY LOCATION AND DESCRIPTION 
The Wendover Pines property  includes two separate assessor parcels located at the northwest 
corner of Highway 260 and Forest Road 504 in  the Heber-Overgaard area of unincorporated  
Navajo County.  The property is generally described as part of Section 15 in Township 12N Range 
16E of the Gila and Salt River base and meridian, Navajo County, Arizona.  See Figure 1, Vicinity 
Map for the location of the proposed Wendover Pines development.   The western parcel is 
comprised of approximately 221 acres and the eastern parcel is comprised of approximately 7 4 
acres.  Both parcels make up the Master Development Plan a rea and will be connected through 
all phases of the development.  The community is surrounded on three sides  (north, east and 
west) by the Apache -Sitgreaves National Forest and the fourth side (southern) by Highway 260 
and private land. 
See Attachment A, Property Recorded Deeds, for a more detailed legal description of the property. 
2.2 TOPOGRAPHY AND DRAINAGE 
The natural terrain of the site is of a gentle rolling nature with several existing drainage ways.  The 
majority of the site has slopes less than 7.5%.  This should allow for easier development of the 
site and the preservation of significant stands of pine trees that exist on the property.  Areas with 
steeper slopes will fall within planned open space areas or on lar ger residential lots where the 
impacts can be minimized.   
The drainage ways within the project area were submitted to the U.S. Corps of Engineers in May 
of 2019 for determination of whether they are under the jurisdiction of Section 404 of the Clean

<!-- page 153 -->
Wendover Pines  Special Development Zone Change Narrative 
Page 2 
 
Water Act.  In a letter from the U.S. Corps of Engineers dated July 3rd, 2019, it was stated that the 
project site did not contain any “waters of the United States” pursuant to 33 CFR Part 325.9. 
2.3 EXISTING LAND USE, COMPREHENSIVE PLAN DESIGNATION AND ZONING 
2.3.1 EXISTING LAND USE 
The property is currently undeveloped land and is surrounded on the north, east and west 
sides by the Apache -Sitgreaves National Forest.  The southern boundary is bordered by 
Highway 260. Across the highway is a s mall commercial use adjacent to the south east 
portion of the property (Circle K convenience market and fueling station) and an existing 
large lot single-family residential community known as High Country Pines.  
2.3.2 EXISTING COMPREHENSIVE PLAN 
The subject property had been identified as within the Heber-Overgaard Area Plan on the 
Navajo County Character Areas Map.   
The Navajo County Comprehensive Plan supports r esidential and commercial 
development within the subject property .  This application is the next step in the land 
planning and development process for this area, creating a variety of housing options and 
local commercial services  that will further the County’s vision of a quality, distinctive 
product that will serve the community.   
2.3.3 EXISTING ZONING 
The Master Development planning area has two different zoning districts on the site.   
The eastern parcel is zoned SD (Special Development) and allows for the development of 
residential subdivisions and other land use projects containing various residential 
dwelling types.   It also allows for deviations from design standards and customized 
development standards.   
The western parcel is currently zoned A-General which currently allows for single-family 
dwellings and a maximum density of 1 du/acre.  The A-General zoning has a maximum lot 
coverage requirement of 50%. See Figure 2, Existing & Proposed Zoning Exhibit. 
The zoning of properties surrounding the site include:  
North:  Unspecified- Apache-Sitgreaves National Forest 
South:  SD (Special Development) 
East:  Unspecified- Apache-Sitgreaves National Forest 
West:  Unspecified- Apache-Sitgreaves National Forest  
2.4 SURROUNDING DEVELOPMENTS, FACILITIES AND SERVICES 
Wendover Pines is surrounded by the Apache -Sitgreaves National Forest to the west, n orth and 
east.  To the north, in the National Forest is Camp Ponderosa Ranch.  There is an exi sting single-
family residential development named High Country Pines to the south.  In addition, there is a 
Circle K convenience  store at the entrance to the  High Country Pines  development. The 
community of Heber-Overgaard is approximately 5-miles to the east and can be easily accessed 
via Arizona Route 260.

<!-- page 154 -->
Wendover Pines  Special Development Zone Change Narrative 
Page 3 
 
Home to spectacular hiking, fishing, huntin g and abundant wildlife, Heber-Overgaard is situated 
atop the Mogollon Rim at an elevation of 6,627 feet.  Retirement and tourism are an important 
part of the Heber -Overgaard economy and the proximity to the Sitgreaves National Forest 
provides ample recreational opportunities.  In addition, there  are multiple community facilities 
including a library, 40-acre park, astronomical observatory, number of athletic facilities: baseball, 
football and Little League fields; basketball, volleyball, tennis and racquet ball courts, and a 9-hole 
regulation length golf course. 
Heber-Overgaard Unified School District serves the local residential population . Mountain 
Meadows Primary School (grades Pre -K- through 3rd grade), Capps Middle School (grades 4 -6), 
Mogollon Junior High School (grades 7 -8), and Mogollon Hig h School (grades 9 -12) serve the 
community. Northland Pioneer College, a state -accredited community college, serves Navajo 
County remotely via satellite. The college has centers located in Holbrook, Show Low, 
Snowflake/Taylor, and Winslow.  
The community is served by the Navajo County Sheriff's Department and the Department of Public 
Safety. The Heber-Overgaard Fire Department was founded on Feb. 1, 1971. The fire department 
has 3 paramedics, 5 IMETs, 15 EMTs, and 40 volunteers. 
There are overhead utilities  along a line of wooden poles that parallel Highway 260 located 
approximately 400 feet north of Highway 260.  These overhead lines include electric owned by 
Navopache Electric Cooperative and fiber optic lines owned by Citizens Telecom.  Secondary lines 
from these main overhead lines run southward to serve the existing Circle K store and High 
Country Pines development. 
In addition to the secondary electric and telecom lines, there is an existing water line that runs 
northward under Highway 260 from the Circle K and then turns easterly along the main overhead 
electric/telecom easement to Forest Road 504, which then parallels the Forest Road to serve 
Camp Ponderosa Ranch to the north. 
There are no existing sewer facilities within or immediately adjacent to the property. 
 
3.0 DESCRIPTION OF REQUEST 
3.1 PROJECT OVERVIEW AND SPECIAL DEVELOPMENT ZONING REQUEST 
The Wendover Pines community will feature beautiful new homes on up to 2-acre homesites on 
a 295-acre parcel that is surrounded by forest lands that have been preserved and dedicated as 
permanent natural open space .  Walking trails meander along wooded and open green space, 
past several neighborhood “nodes” and through a central community park with amenities.  The 
community streetscape start s at the entrance and extends through the wooded homesites 
throughout the development.  A variety of uses and densities will be featured  throughout the 
community from rental cabins in the Commercial/Residential district as well as 7,000 square foot, 
10,000 square foot, ½-acre, and 1-acre single family lots, and 2-acre ranchettes.   
A maximum of 450 units will be provided for rent or ownership  within the development.  There 
will be rental units  located adjacent to the Commercial p arcel on the site.  This Commercial-
Residential area will be located at the south easterly main entrance and will feature an attractive 
stone column of approximately 8’ +/- in height with neighborhood identification markers.

<!-- page 155 -->
Wendover Pines  Special Development Zone Change Narrative 
Page 4 
 
 
3.2 MASTER DEVELOPMENT PLAN 
Wendover Pines Master Development Plan is a c onceptual plan that incorporates the natural 
topography and existing natural resources of the site to ensure a high-quality residential master 
planned community that beautifully integrates the local culture and native materials of the 
community. There will be a variety of uses including mixed residential, Commercial and cabin 
rental.   There will be a v ariety of densities and product types available for residents to choose 
from, serving a variety of lifestyle choices within the cool pines of Heber-Overgaard.  At the time 
of platting, a specific product type will be designated for each parcel on a phased basis. See Figure 
3, Conceptual Development Plan for the conceptual layout of the community . While the 
Conceptual Development Plan indicate s preliminary parcel boundaries and residential district  
designations, all residential parcels and districts are subject to change within the plan with out 
triggering an amendment to the zoning, so long as the overall residential unit count is maintained 
at a maximum of 450  total units at full build out . This flexibility is necessary to adapt to actual 
market conditions, residential demand and infrastructure analysis at the time of platting for each 
phase of development. 
The development will create a distinct theme and feel that will remain consistent throughout the 
community and respect the natural character of the region .  This will be accomplished by 
designing the roads and homesites to respond to the existing site topography and vegetation.  
There will be themed entry roads with varying medians that preserve significant stands of the 
Ponderosa Pines that define the area.  There will be thematic elements al ong entry roads that 
help identify the character of the Development.   
Wendover Pines will incorporate character-defining design elements including natural pathways 
that meander along the entry roads and connect to the natural surface trails throughout the  
community.  There will be a vegetative open space buffer along AZ- 260 to reinforce the natural 
aesthetic of the area along with a split rail and stone decorative fence.  The eastern entry drive is 
planned to terminate on the large central open space amenity.  
A 30’ perimeter building setback/buffer shall be provided along the perimeter of the property 
where immediately adjacent to National Forest, providing an appropriate buffer between all 
buildings within the development and the National Forest land. Perimeter walls, landscaping and 
backyards are permitted within this buffer area, but no structures or walls over 8’ in height shall 
be permitted.   
3.3 CIRCULATION AND ACCESS 
Wendover Pines will have two primary access points off AZ-260 into and out of the community  
that align with the existing access points for the High Country Pines community.  There will be a 
secondary access road connection on either side of FR 504 where it crosses the north east corner 
of the property.    
Both vehicular and pedestrian connectivity throughout the community is a critical design feature 
of Wendover Pines in order to link residents to one another and provide ample fire and emergency 
services to and from the property.  A well-planned comprehensive network of local roads will be 
provided that link the entr y drives from one end of the community to the other , connecting 
neighborhoods together through local roads.

<!-- page 156 -->
Wendover Pines  Special Development Zone Change Narrative 
Page 5 
 
Required turn lane  improvements to Highway 260 are being coordinated with the Arizona 
Department of Transportation (ADOT) for the addition of the fourth leg of the existing “T” 
intersections at Cloud Nine Boulevard (westerly access) and at Mainline Road (easterly access). 
No improvements are envisioned to be necessary at the existing intersection of Highway 260 and 
Forest Road 504. 
3.4 PARKS, TRAILS AND OPEN SPACE 
The Project is centered around the existing natural beauty and resources on the site.  The center 
of the site will showcase a central park amenity of approximately 6.5 acres that will be a place to 
experience nature and enjoy recreational activit ies.  The central amenity will include a large 
ramada, fire pits, BBQ grills and outdoor game activities that may include ping pong, corn hole, 
horseshoes, croquet, gaga ball, and/or a half-court basketball court.   
Natural surface t rails that seamlessly blend in to the surroundings will meander through the 
community.  The trails will be located along streets, through open spaces and dedicated trail 
easements that run along the perimeter of lots.  Trail “nodes” will be provided along the trail as 
rest stops and will feature seating so that hikers can catch a breath, bird watch or enjoy the natural 
beauty of the site. Residents will be able to spend hours covering the network of trails and open 
spaces that are proposed with this development. 
In addition to the large, central park amenity and rest stop nodes, on -lot open space will be 
provided.  Residents will feel truly immersed in their natural surroundings with ample on-lot and 
shared open space throughout the development. The developer is working with the Forest Service 
to evaluate the option of improving trails in the forest surrounding the Property for use by the 
general public.  If this can be accomplished, there may be two public access points to the 
surrounding forest lands ; one to the north and one to the west.  No private access to the 
surrounding forest lands will be allowed from individual lots.  
3.5 TREE PRESERVATION PLAN 
The natural landscape and native vegetation are two of the greatest assets of Wendover Pines.  
Ponderosa Pines are abundant throughout portions of the site and the goal is to preserve and 
incorporate as many of these into the development plan as appropriate and practical. The overall 
design of all roads and home-sites will respond to significant stands of pines and will be evaluated 
during final design of each phase. 
3.6 POTABLE WATER SERVICE STATEMENT 
CVL Consultants wrote a preliminary water master plan is to identify existing conditions and 
determine the water infrastructure requirements for the proposed Wen dover Pines 
development. It also determine d the anticipated Wendover Pines needs for an on-site well, 
booster pumps, and storage tank fa cilities. The water distribution network will be designed per 
current guidelines set forth by the Arizona Department of Environmental Quality’s (ADEQ’s) 
Engineering Bulletins No. 8 & 10, dated August 1978 and May 1978. 
Water demands for Wendover Pines are based on total dwelling units. The development is an 
approximate 295 acre proposed residential development with approximately 4 acres of 
commercial. There are 450 proposed single -family residential units planned, ranging from small 
cabin sites to 2 acre lots. 
The calculated water demands for Phase 2 (Build-out) are as follows:

<!-- page 157 -->
Wendover Pines  Special Development Zone Change Narrative 
Page 6 
 
Average Day Demand:                    135.9 gpm  
Peak Day Demand:       337.2 gpm  
 
Phasing of the water system for the project will allow for expansion as needed to meet the 
requirements of each phase. 
A WaterCAD model was developed to ensure Wendover Pines’ water distribution network meets 
all water requirements. Further details for t he project’s proposed water distribution system can 
be found in the Preliminary Water Master Plan prepared by CVL Consultants dated 11/08/19. 
 
3.7 SEWER SERVICE STATEMENT 
Sewer service will be provided to the Wendover Pines development by either a district formed to 
own and operate the system or private operators.  The purpose of the wastewater analysis is to 
identify existing conditions and determine the wastewater  requirements for the proposed 
Wendover Pines development. This wastewater master plan outlines a collection and conveyance 
network needed to connect the gravity and low- pressure mains to the treatment facility. The 
wastewater collection and conveyance network will be designed per current guidelines set forth 
by the Arizona Department of Environmental Quality’s (ADEQ’s) Engineering Bulletin No. 12, 
dated July 1978. 
Where gravity flow to the Treatment Plant is not possible due to site topography, a low pressure 
force main will be utilized  with an individual grinder pump per home. Wastewater flows for 
Wendover Pines are based on total dwelling units  and Commercial requirements . The 
development is an approximate 29 5 acre proposed residential development with 4 acres of 
commercial development. There are 450 proposed single-family residential units planned, ranging 
in lot size from cabins to 2 acre lots.  
Numerous treatment technolo gies are available for treatment of the Wendover Pines influent 
from a wide array of manufacturers.  For lower capacity system of less than 1,000,000 gpd, 
package systems will likely be the most economical.  
The wastewater treatment plant (WWTP) will be co nstructed in phases as development of 
Wendover Pines progresses.  The WWTP will be designed for a build-out average dry weather flow 
of peak month of 142,000 gpd with an instantaneous hydraulic peak flow of 290,000 gpd. 
Further details for the project’s proposed sanitary sewer network can be found in the Wastewater  
Master Plan prepared by CVL Consultants dated 11/06/19. 
3.8 DRAINAGE STATEMENT 
This Drainage Statement provides preliminary hydrologic analysis for the proposed Wendover 
Pines development. In addition, the narrative  addresses the stormwater requirements as stated 
by Navajo County.  This section is focused on providing preli minary design information, 
evaluation, and analysis for statistical flood events up to and including the 100 -year storm per 
Navajo County standards .  The procedures used herein are derived from, and performed with, 
currently accepted engineering methodologies and practices.   
The site is in  Navajo County within Section 15 North, Township 12N, Range 16E of the Gila and 
Salt River Base and Meridian, Navajo County, Arizona.  The site encompasses approximately 295

<!-- page 158 -->
Wendover Pines  Special Development Zone Change Narrative 
Page 7 
 
acres.  The Navajo County, Arizona and Incorporated Areas Flood Insurance Rate Map (FIRM), 
panel numbers 04017C4115F, 04017C4150F and 04017C4161F Map Revised November 4, 2015 
[5], indicates the site falls within Zone X. 
The overall drainage concept proposed is to provide detention basins located within the low-lying 
‘ravine’ areas of the site to meet pre versus post -developed flow conditions.  Preliminary basin 
sizing was based on the volume difference for the 100 -year, 2 -hour storm.  The sub -basin 
delineations are based on the existing topography.  Ref er to Plate 1 in the preliminary drainage 
calculation for sub -basin delineations and detention basin locations.  The precipitation data is 
based on NOAA Atlas 14 Point Precipitation Frequency Estimates (P = 2.89 inches).  Runoff 
coefficients are per the AD OT Highway Drainage Design Manual Hydrology for Arizona 
Department of Transportation, March 1993 in accordance with Navajo County Drainage Policy.  
The runoff coefficients were extracted from Figure 2-3 using the moderate urban area land type 
and from Figure 2-5 for the undeveloped area land type.  Soil type is D, 50% vegetation cover. The 
runoff coefficient values are per the Table below. 
 
No soil information has been made available in the NRCS website for the Wendover Pines area at 
this time. Please see Attachment C, Preliminary Drainage Calculations, for additional information. 
3.9 OTHER UTILITIES AND SERVICES (POWER, FIBER, CABLE, WASTE DISPOSAL) 
The Wendover Pines development will provide electrical service and telecom/data service to each 
lot utilizing the existing facilities located along the main overhead lines already present on site.  
Frontier does have fiber and copper adjacent to the site, and t his will be a fiber subdivision with 
high speed internet and telephone capabilities. Direct TV and Dish Network are the current cable 
television options; both require individual satellite service.  
Waste disposal will be provided fo r both the residential and commercial components of th e 
project. This will be contracted through a  local waste management service provider in the area, 
and details related to the methods of waste disposal will be determined prior to the first phase of 
development.  
3.10 PROJECT PHASING 
The Wendover Pines development will be constructed in several phases, with the size and location 
of each phase to be determined based on market demand and project absorption. Prior to each 
phase of the development commencing, a detailed analysis  of the uses , densities, utilities and 
transportation infrastructure requirements will be provided to Navajo County as part of the 
preliminary platting/site planning review process  to ensure that each phase provides  the 
appropriate utilities and services needed to support it.

Wendover Pines  Special Development Zone Change Narrative 
Page 8 
 
 
4.0 DEVELOPMENT STANDARDS 
Part of the Special Development zoning process is to identify customized land uses and development 
standards that establish reasonable development parameters and create and preserve a livable 
community.  Outlined below are customized development standards and allowed uses for the varying 
“districts” that are included in the Master Development Plan.   At the time of preliminary plat, the 
developer will identify the limits of each applicable parcel and the specific district and  development 
standards that the parcel will conform to along with the phased utility and infrastructure analysis as 
required.  
4.1 LAND USE/ZONING DESIGNATIONS 
The development standards for the Master Development Plan will vary by “district” and are based 
on the parameters of the assigned area.  Each district will also dictate the allowed land uses and 
density as provided below. 
4.1.1 SINGLE-FAMILY ZONING DISTRICTS 
WP-7 Single-Family Residential Zoning District Min. 7,000 sq/ft lot 
WP-10 Single-Family Residential Zoning District Min. 10,000 sq/ft lot  
WP-20 Single-Family Residential Zoning District Min. 20,000 sq/ft lot  
WP-40 Single-Family Residential Zoning District Min. 40,000 sq/ft lot  
Allowed Uses  
• Single-family dwellings --including conventional site -built homes, 
Manufactured/Modular Homes and Factory Built Buildings.  For the purposes of this 
development, Manufactured/Modular Homes are defined as homes that are either 
entirely or partially constructed off site and transported on -site to be permanently 
placed.  Manufactured/modular homes shall be installed on a permanent block 
foundation or anchoring such as piers and footings, and methods of construction that 
support and secure the manufactured home to the ground. The mounting system 
must be concealed such that the home’s appearance most closely resembles a “site-
built home.” 
• Public, elementary, high and pri vate schools with a curriculum the same as 
customarily given in public schools. 
• Utility facilities -- facilities for the delivery to the public, by a regulated public or 
private utility or a public or private entity, of water, gas, electricity, steam, hot or cold 
air, telecommunications and cable television service, or sewer service. This shall

Internal Capture 
I t  i s  e x p e c t e d  t h a t  s o m e  t r i p s  b e t w e e n  r e s i d e n t s  a n d  b u s i n e s s e s will occur that do enter the internal 
roadways of the site but do not leave the site. These types of trips are referred to as internally captured 
trips. An internal capture reduction rate was applied to the tr ips from the site to account for vehicles that 
do not enter the ADOT roadway. T he value of this rate is differ ent for the AM peak hour, PM peak hour, 
and Average Daily Trips (ADT). The rates were calculated using the methodology presented in the National 
Cooperative Highway Research Program Report 684 . These reductions were applied only to the trips 
attracted by the restaurant land uses. Residents in all lots west of Forest Service Road 504 will have access 
to the restaurants via internal local roads. The Trip Generation Summary table shows the internal capture 
volumes. 
Pass-By and Diverted Link Trips 
Based on the published ITE data, as found in Trip Generation Handbook, 2nd Edition, it can be estimated 
that some portion of the traffic entering and exiting a commerc ial development would come from traffic 
already on the external street system. The term ‘pass-by’ trips refers to traffic already traveling on a study 
roadway from an origin to a destination that stops into the com mercial development on the way. Pass-by 
trips would be expected to occur for this development from vehicles traveling along SR-260. A reduction in 
the through movement volumes at the study intersections was tak en to acc ount f or thi s. The am ount  
reduced was calculated based on ITE data for each land use within the site. The reduction percentage for 
midday weekend trips is expected to be the same as that for PM weekday. The daily weekday reduction 
percentage was assumed as an average of the percentage for AM and PM weekday. The total values of the 
reduction are shown in the Trip Generation Summary table.

The development is anticipated to generate 3,338 new weekday da ily external trips, 269 AM peak hour 
external trips, 265 PM peak hour external trips, and 268 Saturday midday peak hour external trips. The trip 
generation calculations can be found in the attachments to this document. 
Study Horizon Years 
The criteria for this study will be based on ADOT requirements, under which this development is considered 
a Category I ‘Small Development’ since it is expected to genera te less than 500 trips during either peak 
hour. Per the requirements, the opening year 2020 and the horiz on year 2023, three (3) years after 
opening, will be analyzed. 
Trip Distribution 
Two trip distribution patterns were developed, one for employment and one for population, within a given 
radius of the proposed site. It is expected that the restaurant uses of the proposed development will attract 
trips based on residential properties within a large radius of the site. It is expected that the residential uses 
of the proposed development will generate trips based on employ ment/commercial properties within a 
large radius of the site. Trips are expected to predominantly travel to/from the site and either the Phoenix 
Metro Area (for residential uses), the City of Payson, the City  of Show Low, or the City of Heber. The 
resulting trip distribution percentages for the study area are shown in the site trip distribution table.

Future Roadway Network 
External access to the site will be provided by the construction of yet unnamed internal local roads. These 
roads will intersect SR 260 at Cloud Nine Boulevard and at Mainline Road East as well as intersecting Forest 
Service Road 504 approximately 1,900 feet north of SR 260. Inte rnal roads will provide connection to all 
external access points from all lots.  
Surrounding Developments  
There is an existing residential development of single-family h omes on SR 260 directly south of the 
proposed development site. This development is accessed via Mainline Road and Cloud Nine Boulevard. At 
the south-east corner of the intersection of Mainline Road East and SR 260 is a convenience store with gas 
pumps.

Attachment C
Methodology Overview
This form facilitates trip generation estimation using data within the Institute of Transportation Engineer's (ITE) Trip Generation Manual, 10th Edition and methodology described within ITE's Trip 
Generation Handbook, 3rd Edition. These references will be referred to as Manual and Handbook, respectively. The Manual  contains data collected by various transportation professionals for a 
wide range of different land uses, with each land use category represented by a land use code (LUC). Average rates and equations have been established that correlate the relationship between 
an independent variable that describes the development size and generated trips for each categorized LUC in various settings and time periods. The Handbook  indicates an established 
methodology for how to use data contained within the Manual when to use the fitted curve instead of the average rate and when to adjustments to the volume of trips are appropriate and how to 
do so. The methodology steps are represented visually in boxes in Figure 3.1. This worksheet applies calculations for each box if applicable.
Box 1 - Define Study Site Land Use Type & Site Characteristics
The analyst is to pick an appropriate LUC(s) based on the subject's zoning/land use(s)/future land use(s). The size of the land use(s) is described in reference to an independent variable(s) 
specific to (each) the land use (example: 1,000 square feet of building area is relatively common).

Box 4 - Is Study Site Multimodal?
Per the Handbook, "if the objective is to establish a local trip generation rate for a particular land use or study site, the simplified approach (Box 9) may be acceptable but the Box 5 through 8 
approach is required if the study site is located in an infill setting, contains a mix of uses on-site, or is near significant transit service." 
Box 5/Box 9 - Estimate Baseline Trips/Estimate Vehicular Trips (Determine Equation)
Vehicle trips are estimated using rates/equations applicable to each LUC. When the appropriate graph has a fitted curve, the Handbook  has a process (Figure 4.2) to determine when to use it 
versus using the weighted average rate or collecting local data. The methodology requires for engineering judgement in some circumstances and permits engineering judgement to override or 
make adjustments when appropriate to best project (example 1: study site is expected to operate differently than data in the applicable land use code - such as restaurant that is closed in the 
morning or in the evening; example 2: LUC data in a localized area fails to be represented by the typically selected fitted curve/weighted average rate - a small shop/LUC 820, AM peak hour is 
skewed by the high y-intercept).

If vehicle trip reductions are not applied for internal capture and alternative mode, vehicle trips may be separtated into vehicle trip subsets (pass-by trips, diverted trips, truck trips, new passenger 
vehicle trips) as part of Box 10. If vehicle trip reductions are to be applied, continue to Box 6.
Box 6 - Convert Baseline Vehicle Trips to Person Trips
If no vehicle trip reductions are to be applied, this portion may be ignored. The Handbook  states "There are not enough samples to derive precise percentages by mode...however, for all but one,
...the motor vehicle percentage of total person trips is at least 96 percent." and "[vehicle occupancy for] many of the most commonly analyzed land use codes are not [available]." This form 
assumes that the total baseline vehicle trips for all land use codes accounts for 90% of total person trips. Unless otherwise specified, this form later reverses the conversion in Box 8.

Box 7 - Estimate Internal Person Trips, External Walk/Bike Trips, Transit Person Trips, External Person Trips (Internal Capture)
Internal capture occurs for mixed-use developments when a portion of the trips generated by the site are expected to have the both the origin and destination within the site. Internal capture is 
not dependent on mode choice. The table below presents the internal capure percentages and trips in units of vehicle trips. CivTech can provide trips in units of persons if requested.

Box 7 - Estimate Internal Person Trips, External Walk/Bike Trips, Transit Person Trips, External Person Trips (Alternative Mode)
Alternate mode reductions are applied to account for trips to/from the study site made any means except as the driver of a personal vehicle (though carpooling is separate in Box 9). Alternative  
mode reductions, with respect to trips entering/existing the site, include trips where more than one mode is used as long as the trip is not in a vehicle when crossing the boundary of the study 
site. The reduction is applied as a percent of vehicular trips removed from total external trips. The reduction percentage used does not include any amount of alternate mode trips that are 
accounted for in the baseline rates; the Dense Multi-Urban Use and City Core settings already account for alternate mode trips, though further reduction may still be reasonable in specific 
circumstances. The table below presents the alternative mode percentages and trips in units of vehicle trips. CivTech can provide trips in units of persons if requested.

Box 8 - Convert Person Trips to Final Vehicle Trips
The vehicle occupancy and baseline alternate mode are now factored out from the external trips in vehicles, after any adjustments for internal capture and additional alternate mode from Box 7. 
In Box 6, vehicle trips were considered to account for 90% of total person trips. Alternate mode trips in addition to the baseline, if any, are accounted for in Box 7. It is estimated that vehilcle trips 
should be reduced by an additional 0% due to carpoo ling. The final external trips in vehicles is multiplied by 90% - 0% = 90% to produce the external vehicle trips.

Box 10 - Estimate Vehicle Trip Subsets Pass-by/Diverted Trips, Truck Trips (Pass-By Trips)
Some trips may be classified as "pass-by" trips, where some vehicle trips generated by the study site are already traveling on an adjacent road and make a stop while passing by. These trips do
not add traffic volume to the roadway. The Handbook does not specify that a 'pair' of pass-by trips must enter and exit the same driveway. The current edition of the Handbook  indicates that 
pass-by trips should have directional distribution applied (%in/%out), though reviewers often comment when pass-by trip "pairs" do not occur within a the specified time period. This is likely due 
to ease of calculation and traditional methodology found in the first edition of the Handbook . As such, the analyst may ignore the direction distribution divide the total pass-by trip volume by 2 to 
apply pass-by "pairs". In addition, the analyst may consider pass-by rates at a reduced rate. Data is not available for all land use codes and all periods, assumtions are highlighted. The 
percentage is applied to total external vehicle trips.

1.0 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
The Wendover Pines development is located on the west side of the Heber-Overgaard area in 
Navajo County, Arizona. The development is an approximate 294.5 acre proposed residential 
development including 4 acres of commercial development. There are approximately 450 
proposed residential units planned, ranging in lot size from cabins to 2 acre lots. Wastewater 
services to Wendover Pines will be provided by a proposed on-site wastewater treatment facility 
maintained by a District or private operators. There are no existing sanitary sewer services on-
site.  
 
Preliminary wastewater flow calculations were prepared based on the design requirements of the 
Arizona Department of Environmental Quality’s (ADEQ’s) Engineering Bulletin No. 11, dated 
July, 1978. The estimated wastewater flows are as follows: 
 
Wendover Pines Sewer Generation Rates: 
 Average Daily Flow:                               122,875 gpd  
 Peak Daily Flow:                    285,938 gpd  
 
The project’s sanitary sewer network will meet the wastewater requirements of ADEQ 
engineering bulletins relevant to the project and shall be a combination of gravity lines and 
grinder pumps with low pressure lines. A preliminary sewer capacity analysis was performed to 
ensure Wendover Pines’ gravity sewer network and low pressure sewer network could meet all 
wastewater requirements. See Appendix A for Wendover Pines gravity sewer capacity analysis 
and Appendix B for Wendover Pines low pressure grinder pump analysis. 
 
Treatment of collected sewage will be provided by a package type wastewater treatment plant 
designed to meet ADEQ’s discharge limits for A+ effluent. Treated effluent will be discharged to 
an existing wash located on the northeast corner of the property. All necessary permits from 
Navajo County, ADEQ, and USFS will be obtained.

2.0 INTRODUCTION 
2.1 Project Description 
The Wendover Pines development is located west of the Heber-Overgaard populated area in 
Navajo County, Arizona. The development is an approximate 294.5 acre proposed residential 
development including 4 acres of commercial development. There are approximately 450 
proposed residential units planned, ranging in lot size from cabins to 2 acre lots. This preliminary 
wastewater master plan presents the proposed sanitary sewer network necessary to provide 
wastewater services to Wendover Pines. Wastewater lines and treatment will be maintained by a 
District or private operators.  
 
2.2 Scope of Work 
The purpose of this wastewater analysis is to identify existing conditions and determine the 
wastewater requirements for the proposed Wendover Pines development. This preliminary 
wastewater master plan will outline a sanitary sewer network needed to discharge to the 
proposed Wendover Pines on-site wastewater treatment facility. The sanitary sewer network will 
be designed per current guidelines set forth by the Arizona Department of Environmental 
Quality’s (ADEQ’s) Engineering Bulletin No. 11, dated July, 1978. 
 
2.3 Project Location 
Wendover Pines is located in Navajo County, Arizona and is surrounded by the Sitgreaves 
National Forest on the West, North, and East sides, with Arizona State Highway 260 to the 
South. The project is approximately 3 miles northwest of Heber-Overgaard. The project is 
located in Township 12 North, Range 16 East, Section 15 of the Gila and Salt River Base and 
Meridian, Navajo County, Arizona. See Figure 1 (Page 4) for a Vicinity Map and Figure 2 (Page 
5) for a Location Map.   
 
2.4 Land Use and Population 
The land is currently undeveloped. A summary of the land use an d dwelling units within 
Wendover Pines is presented in Table 1. The Conceptual Site Layout Bubble Plan is presented in 
Figure 3 (Page 6). Preliminary wastewater demands for Wendover Pines are based on proposed 
total residential dwelling units and commercial acreage.

2.5 Topographic Conditions 
Wendover Pines encompasses approximately 294.5 acres of undeveloped land. The land 
decreases in elevation generally from the southwest to the northeast. The total elevation change 
is approximately 269 feet, dropping from 6,835 feet above mean sea level (MSL) near the 
southwest corner of the development to about 6,566 feet MSL at the northeast corner of the 
development. The site has a series of small washes across the site that were determined by the 
US Army Corps of Engineers to be non-jurisdictional under Section 404 of the Clean Water Act.

3.0 WASTEWATER NETWORK DESIGN CRITERIA 
The following are design criteria used to prepare this preliminary wastewater master plan. 
Criteria complies with ADEQ’s Engineering Bulletin No.11 (July, 1978). Any deviation from the 
listed design standards shall be noted in this preliminary wastewater master plan. 
 
3.1 Design Criteria 
o Average Daily Flow Unit Factor for residential Single-Family 
 75 gpcd, 3.5 people per DU 
o Peak Flow Peaking Factor 
 PF = 2.3, based on total population and Figure 9-1. See Appendi x C for ARS 
R18-9-E301 4.01 D Sewage Flow Peaking Standard. 
 Commercial PF = 3.0 
o Average Daily Flow Unit Factor for Commercial 
 0.5 gallons per day per square foot 
o Sewer Pipe Flow Depth: d/D ≤ 0.70 
o Velocities, determined using Manning’s formula ቂ𝑉 ൌ

o Minimum Slopes 
 8-inch = 0.0034 ft/ft 
 10-inch = 0.0026 ft/ft

o Collection lines shall have a m inimum drop of 0.20 feet across manholes for sewers with 
intersecting pipe angles between 45 and 90 degrees. Minimum dro p of 0.10 feet for 
intersecting pipe angles less than 45 degrees. 
o Maximum Manhole Spacing 
 Less than or equal to a 12-inch diameter pipe is 400 feet 
o Manhole size 
 5-foot Manholes are required when : the pipe size is greater tha n 12-inch in diameter, 
there are two or more inlets into the manhole, when drop sewer connection is designed 
(2.5 feet or more above the inlet), and for depths greater than 12-feet. 
 Water tight manholes are required for any manhole located in a floodplain, wash, or other 
area subject to storm water runoff. 
o Minimum Cover 
 4-foot of cover measured from subgrade to top of pipe. If there  is no pavement then 
finished grade to top of pipe.

5.0 EXISTING AND PROPOSED INFRASTRUCTURE 
5.1 Existing Sewer Mains 
There are no existing sewer mains within the Wendover Pines property limits.  
 
5.2 Proposed On-Site Wastewater Infrastructure 
A portion of the Wendover Pines development will be serviced via gravity sewer. The areas 
where topographic conditions restrict the use of gravity sewer, a low pressure force main will be 
utilized. These areas and a representative wastewater collection system layout are shown in 
Figure 4 (Page 13). 
 
5.3 Proposed Gravity Sewer Network 
A system of 8-inch gravity sewer lines will connect the low pressure sewer network to the 
wastewater treatment plant located at the northeast portion of the Wendover Pines development. 
Due to topographic conditions, an estimated 113 units have the capability to be served by gravity 
without the need of an individual grinder pump per unit. The sewer system layout will be revised 
as needed to reflect the proposed layout. 
 
As Wendover Pines is developed and final lotting established, a determination will be made as to 
whether each lot will be on gravity service or require an individual grinder pump connected to 
the low pressure system. 
 
5.4 Proposed Low Pressure Sewer Network 
Each of the estimated 337 units served through the low pressure system will require an 
individual grinder pump system for each home with a low pressure sewer main system in the 
proposed roadways. The low pressure sewer main system will outfall to terminal sewer manholes 
and then discharge into the gravity sewer network. As Wendover Pines is developed and final 
lotting established, the design of the low pressure system will be completed.

<!-- page 223 -->
Wendover Pines  Coe & Van Loo Consultants, Inc.  
Preliminary Wastewater Master Plan  Project No.: 1.14.0334301

N:\14 Coe and Van Loo II LLC\0334301\Enviro\Reports\Wendover.Pines.WWMP.docx              November 6, 2019 
6.0 WASTEWATER TREATMENT  
Numerous treatment technologies are available for processing of  the Wendover Pines influent 
from a wide array of manufacturers.  For a lower capacity syste m of less than 1,000,000 gpd, 
package systems may be the most economical. A schematic wastewa ter treatment plant layout is 
provided in Figure 5 (Page 14) at an elevation of 6,566 feet, the low point of the property. 
 
6.1 Influent Characteristics 
The wastewater treatment plant (WWTP) may be constructed in pha ses as development of 
Wendover Pines progresses.  Influent characteristics are likely to be in line with domestic values 
typical in Arizona.  Influent ch aracteristics assumed for Wendo ver Pines are summarized in 
Table 3. 
 
Table 3 – Typical Influent Characteristics 
 
Characteristics Build-out 
Flows:  
- Average Dry Weather Flow of Peak Month1, gpd 142,000 
- Instantaneous Hydraulic Peak, gpd 290,000 
- pH 6.0 – 9.0 
  
Concentrations:  
- Biochemical Oxidation Demand (BOD5), mg/L 400 
- Total Suspended Solids (TSS), mg/L 300 
- Total Kjeldahl Nitrogen (TKN), mg/L 50 
- Ammonia Nitrogen (NH3-N), mg/L 40 
- Total Nitrogen (Total N), mg/L 50 
- Total Phosphorus (Total P), mg/L 10 
- Alkalinity, mg/L CaCO3 250 
  
Loadings:  
- BOD5, lbs/day 417 
- TSS, lbs/day 313 
- Total N, lbs/day 52 
- Total P, lbs/day 10 
  
Liquid Temperatures:  
- Winter  12°C (54°F) 
- Summer  30°C (85°F) 
1Average Day Weather Flow of Peak Month = Average Day Flow x 1.15 
 
6.2 Effluent Quality Standards 
The effluent quality goal is to meet or exceed the requirements  f o r  C l a s s  A +  a n d  t h e  B e s t  
Available Demonstrated Control Technology (BADCT) as required b y ADEQ.  The target 
concentrations for the design of the WWTP are summarized in Table 4.
